It does this by "broadcasting" the smaller array to match the shape of the larger array, thus allowing element-wise operations between them.\nThe basic idea of broadcasting is to align arrays with different shapes so that they can be operated on element-wise. Here are the basic rules applied for broadcasting:\n1. **Matching Shape from Right to Left**: If one array has a dimension of size 1, it can be broadcasted to any size in that dimension to match the other array, provided the other dimensions match from right to left.\n2. **Size 1 Dimensions**: If one array has fewer dimensions than the other, size 1 dimensions are added on the left until both arrays have the same number of dimensions.\n3. **Singleton Dimensions**: Operations over a single element are done by replicating it across the dimensions that don\'t exist.\n\nLet\'s look at an example', token_ids=[198, 4755, 14149, 49927, 374, 264, 8147, 4668, 304, 279, 16610, 14149, 6875, 369, 13325, 430, 6276, 499, 311, 2804, 7677, 389, 18893, 449, 2204, 21483, 323, 12562, 13, 1102, 1587, 420, 555, 330, 55262, 287, 1, 279, 9333, 1358, 311, 2489, 279, 6211, 315, 279, 8294, 1358, 11, 8617, 10923, 2449, 45539, 7677, 1990, 1124, 627, 791, 6913, 4623, 315, 49927, 374, 311, 5398, 18893, 449, 2204, 21483, 779, 430, 814, 649, 387, 24026, 389, 2449, 45539, 13, 5810, 527, 279, 6913, 5718, 9435, 369, 49927, 512, 16, 13, 3146, 65530, 23342, 505, 10291, 311, 14043, 96618, 1442, 832, 1358, 706, 264, 13167, 315, 1404, 220, 16, 11, 433, 649, 387, 13195, 291, 311, 904, 1404, 304, 430, 13167, 311, 2489, 279, 1023, 1358, 11, 3984, 279, 1023, 15696, 2489, 505, 1314, 311, 2163, 627, 17, 13, 3146, 1730, 220, 16, 33123, 96618, 1442, 832, 1358, 706, 17162, 15696, 1109, 279, 1023, 11, 1404, 220, 16, 15696, 527, 3779, 389, 279, 2163, 3156, 2225, 18893, 617, 279, 1890, 1396, 315, 15696, 627, 18, 13, 3146, 27004, 33123, 96618, 25927, 927, 264, 3254, 2449, 527, 2884, 555, 29641, 1113, 433, 4028, 279, 15696, 430, 1541, 956, 3073, 382, 10267, 596, 1427, 520, 459, 3187], cumulative_logprob=None, logprobs=None, finish_reason=length, stop_reason=None)]

Request 91 - Prompt tokens: 35, Output tokens: 18601actual_outputs: [CompletionOutput(index=0, text='  \nAzures provides a logical grouping of every element used in a specific app or a solution named as **Resource Groups**. Everything that is needed to run an application can be kept together including storage accounts, databases, virtual networks, and the application itself. It keeps permissions or security roles same within the whole app which is logical and easy to manage. Everything belonging to this group can be viewed, managed or monitored individually or in the groups at required. In terms of billing, it helps in calculating total cost of the app as all related charges are grouped together.\n\n**App Service Plans** is how Azure determines what kind of VirtualMachine and charge model is required to run the app. Each app service plan defines what level of computing resources should be allocated such as memory, CPU, instance counts. This plan is the key to unlock various Azure features like scalability and load balancing. Azure uses it to decides whether you want a shared VM, a dedicated VM, or perhaps isolated.\n\nThe relationship between **App Service Plans** and **Resource Groups**: They are designed to hold App Service plans which further can host several web apps, each having its setting and configurations as per requirement. However an App Service plan and a Resource group both can be mapped to many. One app service plan can belong to one resource group but one resource group can have many app service plans. Moreover multiple web apps can be attached to one app service plan and also they can be a member of same group. However each App Service Plan can be put into only one resource group.\n\nThe relationship between **App Service Plans** and **Web Apps**: As app service plan is created, you can make use of this plan to deploy one ore more web apps. This is achieved by dividing the large chunks of work into smaller ones and executing them simultaneously. To perform a case-sensitive sort, you have a couple of options:\n\n### 1. Use the `localeCompare()` method\n\nThe `localeCompare()` method returns a number indicating whether the calling string comes before, after, or is the same as the string it\'s being compared to, in the sort order. When used with the `sort()` method, it allows for case-sensitive sorting.\n\n```javascript\nconst arr = ["Z", "a", "B", "y"];\narr.sort((a, b) => a.localeCompare(b, undefined, {sensitivity: \'case\'}));\nconsole.log(arr); // Output: ["B", "Z", "a", "y"]\n```\n\nHowever, note that older browsers (e.g., Internet Explorer) might not support the `localeCompare()` options like `{sensitivity: \'case\'}`. When you deploy your application to Azure App Service, you're working with several entities, such as resource groups, plans, and web applications. Understanding the relationships between these entities is crucial for managing your applications effectively.\n\n### Resource Groups\n\nA **resource group** is a logical container that holds related resources for an application. It serves as a way to organize and manage resources, such as web apps, databases, storage, and networking components, that are required to support an application. Resource groups provide a scope for applying role-based access control (RBAC), tags, and policies. You can consider a resource group as a folder that contains all the resources needed to run your application.\n\n### App Service Plans\n\nAn **App Service Plan** (also known as a plan) defines the resources and scaling settings for your web application. It specifies the number of instances, instance size, scaling rules, and features like autoscaling, load balancing, and secure sockets layer (SSL) certificates. Plans are essentially the pricing tier and compute resources allocated to your web application. Different plans offer varying levels of CPU, memory, storage, and features, allowing you to choose the one that best fits your application's requirements and budget.\n\n### Web Applications\n\nA **web application** in Azure App Service refers to the actual web app, mobile app, or API application that you deploy. Web applications are the core entities that Azure App Service hosts and manages. Each web application is associated with a specific App Service Plan, which determines its underlying compute resources and scalability options.\n\n### Relationships\n\n- **Resource Group and Web Application**: A web application is deployed within a resource group. To speed up the computation, data is broken down into smaller chunks, and each chunk is processed concurrently on numerous computers, processors, or cores.\nWhy Parallel Computing:\nBy tackling difficult computation-intensive issues, parallel computing aids in overcoming the limits of sequential computing. Real-time data processing, complex simulations, and big data analysis are all examples of tasks that can benefit from parallel computing.\nGoals of Parallel Computing:\nSome of the objectives of parallel computing include the following:\nAcceleration: Enhance the speed with which computations are executed\nScalability: Handle vast volumes of data and increase the complexity of computations\nEfficiency: Minimizing power consumption and increasing resource utilization\nReliability: Lowering the likelihood of computation failure\nApplications of Parallel Computing:\nScientific Computing: computational fluid dynamics, weather forecasting, and quantum chemistry, for example\nMachine Learning: deep learning, neural networks, and natural language processing are examples\nData Analytics: data mining, data processing, and data analysis in general, for instance\nGaming: simulations of physics, graphics processing, and rendering\nCryptography: Cracking an encryption and cyber security, for example\nAdvantages of Parallel Computing:\nFaster computation times: Quick execution of computationally intensive jobs\nScalability: Handling large data and sophisticated computations\nCost-effective: reducing the need for individual, expensive computers\nImproved efficiency: optimizing resource utilization and minimizing power consumption\nLimitations of Parallel Computing:\nSynchronization difficulties: coordinating and controlling concurrent processes\nCommunication overheads: transmitting data between processors\nLimited parallelism: some programs cannot be sufficiently parallelized\nLoad balance: ensuring fair workload distribution\nDebugging complexity: pinpointing and isolating problems in parallel programs\nParallel computing is a potent tool for tackling difficult computational issues, though it also has its limits.
